# Playbook for KA220 Youth template question

This user model guides content preparation; it is not an official verified form. The internal title declares 2025 and the user confirmed the Youth sector and the KA220-YOU stock. The rules referred to here are those 2026 of the PDFs provided, to be checked against actual calls, updates and forms before use.

Coverage: 721 main paragraphs, 122 tables, 186 rows, 347 cells, 98 field diagrams, 346 field occurrences. All text, including empty boxes and repetitions, is in the [full extract].

## Limits and criteria

A limit confirmed here is confirmed in the user model, not in the online2026 form. Component budgets are editorial proposals with a 5% reserve; they are not official separate limits. Where the maximum is not present, it is not estimated.

The official KA220 grid considers Relevance25, Design30, Partnership20, Impact25; at least70 overall and at least13/15/10/13 in groups. The sub-criteria guide the judgment without independent votes. The question→subcriterion map is a reasoned editorial interpretation, separate from the official sentences and attributions of the template.

Expert guidance applicable to actions managed by national agencies; do not automatically transfer directories to Sport centralized management or ESC. For European NGOs/EACEA check the specific route and form.

## Conflitti e cautele rilevati

- **template2025-not-verified2026**: P2 declares2025; the model belongs to the user, without proof of officiality or validity of the 2026 form.
- **management20-percent-conflict**: T57R1C1 and T115R1C1 deny/relativize the management cap and suggest shares above 20%; Programme2026 p243 and expert p49 establish a maximum of 20%. Keep conflict, discard suggested tolerances.
- **historical-priorities**: T33 brand SCH/VET/ADU not updated; Program references pages200–206 are historical. ScreenshotP214 contains old examples, not priorities to apply2026.
- **wp-count-recommendation**: The template includes WP2–WP6, five implementation WPs; Program p243 recommends a maximum of four. Do not convert the recommendation into a ban.
- **repeated-wp2-starters**: T74/T84/T94/T104 report All activities in WP2 also in sections WP3–WP6: avoid verbatim copying.
- **duplicate-box-id**: P377 and P382 both use BOX IMPLEMENTATION-07 for participation and amount management. The stable IDs of the JSON are distinct.
- **template-mandatory-claim**: T7 and copies indicate financial manager mandatory for coordinators: template statement, not verified official obligation of the form.
- **annex-status-unverified**: The eight annexes are editorial playbooks: mandatory, uploadable and unproven limits. They do not replace answers in the form.
- **nonfactual-examples**: Youth17–24, focus groups/hackathons, past cooperation and GDPR commitments are examples or model sentences; they do not describe the user's project.
- **no-automatic-centralized-transfer**: The KA220-x model and expert guidance from national agencies do not authorize automatic transfer to ESC, Sport EACEA or other centralized calls.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T6R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
General description of the organisation…
Main goal of the organisation or mission or vision
Main activities
Core values of the organisation

Target groups
Yearly number of learners….

Partners we work with

Number of paid/unpaid staff …

Members of the group (if you have some members)

Primary criterion:
Relevance of the project
Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements
The evaluators assess in particular:
relevance of the organisation’s profile and mission
alignment between organisational activities and project scope
capacity and experience to contribute to the project
credibility and operational readiness

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear, factual and project-relevant presentation of the organisation, showing that it has the experience, capacity and mandate to contribute effectively to the project.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Organisational profile
Describe:
legal form and type of organisation
mission and main purpose
Keep it factual and concise
✔ Evaluators want to understand who you are quickly.

2. Scope of work and areas of activity
Describe:
main fields of work
target groups you usually engage with
types of activities delivered
Focus on areas relevant to the project
✔ Avoid listing unrelated activities.

3. Experience and expertise
Highlight:
relevant experience (national, European or international)
expertise linked to the project theme
Emphasise practice over prestige
✔ Relevance beats reputation.

4. Organisational capacity
Provide approximate figures for:
paid staff
unpaid staff / volunteers
learners, members or beneficiaries
Keep numbers realistic and rounded
✔ This reassures evaluators about feasibility.

5. Role in the project
Briefly explain:
why the organisation is involved
what it contributes to the partnership
Link profile to project role
✔ This strengthens coherence.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Writing a marketing brochure
Overloading with history or awards
Including irrelevant activities
Not linking profile to the project

What Increases the Score
Clear and structured presentation
Strong alignment with project scope
Demonstrated capacity and experience
Credible role within the partnership
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T7R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Relevant experience: ….
Activities and experience of the organisation in the areas relevant for the project


Key staff:
Skills and/or expertise of key persons involved in the project - manager and people according to the given role

Project manager - 
Financial manager (mandatory for coordinators) - 
Expert in implementation - can be trainer, researcher, psychologist, social worker or whatever is needed for the project implementation
Expert in implementation - …

Please keep the same structure of the descriptions of the key staff like: Name - position - diploma and certificates - expertise and experience (relevant to the project, do not explain details that are not relevant to the project topic)

Primary criterion:
Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements
Relevance of the project
The evaluators assess in particular:
relevance of the organisation’s experience to the project topic
adequacy of skills and expertise of key staff
coherence between organisational profile and assigned role
capacity to deliver planned tasks and results

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and evidence-based description of relevant organisational experience and key staff expertise, demonstrating the organisation’s capacity to implement its role in the project effectively.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Relevant organisational experience
Describe the organisation’s core activities related to the project theme
Focus on:
methods used
target groups addressed
types of results achieved
Mention past projects only if directly relevant
✔ Depth is more important than quantity.

2. Experience in cooperation or projects (if applicable)
Briefly highlight:
experience in collaborative projects
transnational or multi-stakeholder work
Link this experience to the current project’s needs
✔ Shows readiness for KA220 complexity.

3. Skills and expertise of key persons
Describe key persons involved in the project, focusing on:
role in the project
relevant competences and expertise
Keep descriptions functional and role-oriented
✔ Avoid CV-style listings.

4. Complementarity between organisation and staff
Explain how:
organisational experience
individual expertise
work together to support project objectives
✔ Reinforces internal coherence.

5. Contribution to the partnership
Briefly explain:
what the organisation brings to the consortium
why its expertise is necessary
✔ Aligns with partnership logic.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Generic descriptions of experience
Listing unrelated projects or skills
Overly detailed personal biographies
No link between expertise and project tasks

What Increases the Score
Strong relevance to the project topic
Clear alignment between experience and responsibilities
Demonstrated capacity at both organisational and individual level
Concise and focused presentation
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T8R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Implemented projects and initiatives connected with the topic of the project or with the TGs

Description of the associated partners if any and what they will do in the project

Primary criterion:
Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements
Relevance of the project
The evaluators assess in particular:
coherence and continuity of the organisation’s past participation
evidence of learning, improvement and progression
relevance of previous experience to the current proposal
transparency and completeness of information provided

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A concise clarification or contextualisation of past participation, highlighting how previous experience has informed the organisation’s capacity, learning and approach in the current project.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Contextual clarification (if needed)
Use this space to:
clarify the scope or nature of past projects
explain gaps or changes in participation
Keep explanations factual and neutral
✔ This section is optional but strategic.

2. Learning and progression
Briefly explain:
what the organisation learned from previous participation
how this experience improved internal practices or competences
Focus on evolution, not repetition
✔ Evaluators value learning trajectories.

3. Relevance to the current proposal
Explain how past experience:
informs the design of the current project
reduces implementation risks
Avoid repeating content already included elsewhere
✔ Show continuity, not duplication.

4. Capacity strengthening and reliability
If relevant, mention:
improved management capacity
stronger quality assurance
increased ability to cooperate transnationally
✔ This reassures evaluators.

5. Final clarifications (optional)
Add any missing but relevant information that:
does not fit other sections
improves understanding of your profile
✔ Less is more here.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Rewriting the full project history
Repeating organisational description
Defensive explanations
Irrelevant details

What Increases the Score
Clear and honest contextualisation
Demonstrated learning and improvement
Strong link between past experience and current proposal
Concise and purposeful use of the space
```

- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Gaps in current solutions
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Real and shared motivation of partners
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Relevance to applicable objectives and priorities
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Expected change and need for funded cooperation
- [ ] Report missing data and verify crucial sources before making commitments.
- [ ] Check consistency with application, other sections, calendar, roles and budget.

This is an operational checklist of evidence and completeness; it does not require expounding or memorizing internal reasoning.

### Budget caratteri editoriale

| Componente | Massimo suggerito |
|---|---:|
| Concrete problem and recipients in context | 570 |
| Lack of current solutions | 570 |
| Real and shared motivation of partners | 570 |
| Relevance to applicable objectives and priorities | 570 |
| Expected change and need for financed cooperation | 570 |
| Reserve for fittings and overhaul | 150 |
| Maximum total in template | 3000 |

### Critica e verifica richiesta

Problem→gap→answer Is Erasmus+ supported by data? Do not claim that the idea predates the call unless documented.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T36R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
The vision of the project
The vision of our project is 

The reasoning for the project (Identified problems)


Solution:


Benefits and impact


Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and relevance of the problem addressed

consistency with Erasmus+ objectives and priorities

European added value

strength and credibility of the motivation


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear, evidence-based explanation of why this problem matters, why it is not adequately addressed yet, and why Erasmus+ cooperation is the right response.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Problem & need (context-based)
Describe a concrete and current problem

Link it to a specific target group

Base it on real practice or observed gaps

✔ Focus on what does not work well today.

2. Gap in existing approaches
Explain why current solutions are insufficient

Highlight:

fragmentation

lack of practical tools

limited sustainability or impact

✔ This shows maturity and awareness of the field.

3. Motivation of the partnership
Explain why the partners are motivated

Show that the idea:

comes from experience

predates the call

is shared across organisations

✔ Avoid presenting the project as “call-driven”.

4. Alignment with Erasmus+ priorities
Clearly connect the project to 1–2 main priorities

Mention others only if naturally embedded

✔ Be specific, not generic.

5. Why the project deserves funding
Explain the added value of EU funding

Clarify:

who benefits

what changes

what risk exists if the project is not funded

✔ Frame funding as an investment, not support.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Generic societal problems

Copy-paste of Erasmus+ priorities

Overly abstract language

No link between problem and European cooperation


What Increases the Score
Clear cause-effect logic

Problem → gap → Erasmus+ solution

Practical, grounded motivation

Strong sense of necessity
```

### Critica e verifica richiesta

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T37R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
The main goal of the … project is to …

Objectives of the project are:


Tangible results of the project and their impact:


Connection to priorities


Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
 Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and coherence of objectives and results

logical link between needs, objectives, activities and outputs

consistency between objectives and selected Erasmus+ priorities

feasibility and concreteness of expected results


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear set of specific and achievable objectives, translated into concrete, measurable results, with an explicit and credible link to the selected Erasmus+ priorities.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Overall objective (strategic level)
State one clear overarching objective

Directly connected to the core problem identified earlier

Framed at European / systemic level, not only local

✔ This gives direction and coherence to the whole project.

2. Specific objectives (operational level)
Define 3–5 specific objectives

Each objective should be:

focused

action-oriented

realistically achievable within the project duration

✔ Use formulations like “to develop”, “to strengthen”, “to improve”, “to enable”.

3. Concrete results and outputs
For each objective, indicate what will be produced

Results should be:

tangible (frameworks, tools, curricula, methods, pilots, platforms, etc.)

usable beyond the partnership

clearly linked to activities and work packages

✔ Avoid generic results like “increased awareness” without outputs.

4. Link between objectives and Erasmus+ priorities
Explicitly map objectives → priorities

Focus on 1–2 main priorities

Show how priorities are:

embedded in the objectives

reflected in the results

✔ Priorities must be operational, not declarative.

5. Added value of results
Explain:

who will use the results

in which contexts

how they improve current practices

✔ This prepares the ground for impact and sustainability sections.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Objectives that are too broad or abstract

Confusion between objectives and activities

Results not clearly linked to objectives

Priorities mentioned only rhetorically


What Increases the Score
Clear hierarchy: overall objective → specific objectives → results

One-to-one or one-to-many logic between objectives and outputs

Strong internal coherence

Clear and explicit linkage to Erasmus+ priorities
```

Attributions and adjacent steps of the template, preserved as non-normative content:

- [P254]: The project is likely to produce results that will be innovative for its field in general, or for the geographical context in which the project is implemented.
- [P255]: The innovative dimension of a project can relate to the content of the outputs produced by the project, and/or to the working methods applied, and/or to the organisations and persons involved or targeted. (For example it will produce something significantly new in terms of learning opportunities, skills development, access to information, recognition of learning outcomes etc.)
- [P256]: The project will add to the existing knowledge, know-how and/or practices of the organisations and persons involved.
- [P257]: If the application is based on a previous project or existing innovative content, it demonstrates significant added value compared to the previous project results or in terms of new target groups, educational, training or youth activities or geographical spread, and contributes to improving the quality of teaching/learning training in the countries participating in the project. In so far as the initial developer of these previous results is not participating in the project, the relationship between the participating organisations and the initial developer are transparent and respect pre-existing rights.
- [P258]: The proposed innovation or complementarity is proportional to the scale of the project and the experience of the participating organisations.
- [P259]: In case of inclusion projects involving staff or learners with special needs or fewer opportunities, the level of innovation should be considered in relation to the possibilities of the target groups involved.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T38R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Our project offers a unique and innovative approach to …

Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
 Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
level and type of innovation (content, methods, processes, tools)

relevance of innovation in relation to identified needs

contribution beyond existing practices and projects

potential of the innovative elements to be replicated or scaled


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear explanation of what is new or significantly improved compared to existing practices, and why this innovation is meaningful, useful and transferable in the Erasmus+ context.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Define the innovation clearly
State what exactly is innovative

Specify the type of innovation:

methodological

organisational

digital / technological

process-based

Avoid claiming “innovation” in generic terms

✔ Innovation must be explicit and understandable.

2. Innovation in relation to existing practice
Briefly describe what is currently done

Explain how your approach goes beyond it

Focus on:

limitations of current approaches

concrete improvements introduced

✔ Innovation is relative, not absolute.

3. User-centred and needs-driven innovation
Show that innovation:

responds to real user needs

is grounded in practice, not experimentation for its own sake

Emphasise usability and applicability

✔ Evaluators value useful innovation, not technical complexity.

4. Systemic or structural innovation
Explain how the proposal:

changes the way organisations work

improves processes or decision-making

integrates innovation “by design”

✔ This shows long-term relevance.

5. Transferability and scalability
Clarify:

who else can adopt the innovation

under which conditions

with which level of effort

✔ Innovation with reuse potential scores higher.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Claiming innovation without comparison

Describing tools instead of what they change

Over-emphasising technology

Confusing innovation with dissemination


What Increases the Score
Precise definition of “what is new”

Clear contrast with existing practices

Strong link to user needs

Replicability across contexts and countries
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T39R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
 Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
coherence between the project and partners’ previous experience

ability to build on existing initiatives without duplication

clarity of added value compared to past or ongoing work

contribution to continuity and consolidation of results


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear explanation of how the project builds on, extends or connects existing initiatives, creating added value rather than duplicating what partners have already done.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Brief reference to existing initiatives
Mention key relevant initiatives previously or currently carried out by partners

Focus on those directly related to the project topic

✔ Be selective; do not list everything.

2. Limits of existing initiatives
Explain what those initiatives:

achieved

did not fully address

Highlight gaps in:

scale

structure

transferability

sustainability

✔ This justifies the need for a new project.

3. Complementarity and added value
Explain how the project:

connects fragmented experiences

systematises practices

transforms isolated actions into a shared European approach

✔ Show evolution, not repetition.

4. Synergies within the partnership
Clarify how different partners:

bring complementary experiences

build on each other’s previous work

Emphasise knowledge integration, not parallel tracks

✔ This strengthens partnership credibility.

5. Continuity and consolidation
Explain how the project:

capitalises on past results

ensures continuity over time

increases long-term impact

✔ This prepares the ground for sustainability and impact sections.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Long descriptions of past projects without analysis

Claiming complementarity without explanation

Overlapping objectives with previous initiatives

Treating the project as a “new start” disconnected from experience


What Increases the Score
Clear distinction between what existed and what is new

Logical progression from past initiatives to the proposed project

Strong sense of strategic continuity

Evidence of learning from previous experiences
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T40R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
 Impact
The evaluators assess in particular:
capacity to create synergies across fields (education, training, youth, sport)

relevance of the project for more than one field

clarity of mechanisms enabling cross-sectoral impact

potential for systemic or structural effects


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear explanation of how the project connects different fields or strongly impacts at least one of them, showing concrete mechanisms for cross-sectoral learning, transfer and uptake.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Fields addressed by the project
Clearly state:

which fields are directly involved (education, training, youth, sport)

which are indirectly impacted

Avoid claiming all fields unless justified

✔ Precision is valued more than breadth.

2. Synergies between fields
Explain how practices, tools or methods travel across fields

Focus on:

shared competences

transferable methodologies

common challenges

✔ Show interaction, not parallel application.

3. Cross-sectoral mechanisms
Describe concrete mechanisms enabling synergies, such as:

joint frameworks

shared tools or curricula

mixed target groups

collaborative pilots

✔ Evaluators look for “how”, not just “that”.

4. Impact on the addressed fields
Explain what changes in each field:

practices

capacities

quality of interventions

Focus on mid- to long-term effects, not only project activities

✔ Impact should outlive the project.

5. Added value of cross-sectoral approach
Clarify why:

a single-field approach would be insufficient

cooperation across fields increases effectiveness and reach

✔ This reinforces European added value.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Generic statements about “holistic approaches”

Listing fields without explaining interaction

Confusing dissemination with cross-sectoral impact

Claiming impact without mechanisms


What Increases the Score
Clear mapping of fields and interactions

Concrete cross-sectoral processes

Realistic and credible impact pathways

Strong alignment between objectives, activities and fields involved
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T41R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
 Impact
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity of the European added value

necessity of transnational cooperation

extent to which results depend on cross-country collaboration

relevance of results at European level, beyond national contexts


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear justification of why transnational cooperation is essential, showing that the project’s results could not be achieved, or would be significantly weaker, if implemented in a single country.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. European dimension of the problem
Explain how the addressed problem:

exists across multiple countries

affects different national contexts in comparable ways

Avoid presenting it as a purely local issue

✔ The problem must be European in nature or scale.

2. Limits of single-country approaches
Explain why actions at national level:

are fragmented

lack comparability

have limited transferability

Highlight barriers to impact without European cooperation

✔ This creates the rationale for EU-level action.

3. Added value of transnational cooperation
Explain what only a European partnership enables, such as:

comparison of practices

co-creation across contexts

mutual learning and validation

development of shared standards or frameworks

✔ Cooperation must be functional, not symbolic.

4. European-level results
Describe results that:

are applicable across countries

respond to diverse contexts

are designed for reuse and adaptation

Emphasise scalability and transferability

✔ Results should be European “by design”.

5. Contribution to European objectives
Link results to:

Erasmus+ objectives

broader EU priorities (where relevant)

Focus on practical contribution, not policy rhetoric

✔ Show alignment without overclaiming.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Saying “Europe” without explaining why

Confusing dissemination abroad with European added value

Describing mobility instead of cooperation

Treating partners as implementers, not co-creators


What Increases the Score
Clear explanation of why the project cannot work nationally

Strong logic of interdependence between partners

Results designed for multi-country uptake

Convincing European narrative grounded in practice
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T42R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
General needs


Needs of the youths aged 17-24


Needs of youth workers


Needs of participating organisations

Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and accuracy of the needs analysis

relevance of the identified needs for the target groups

consistency between needs, objectives and activities

alignment with Erasmus+ priorities and policy context


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear, well-argued identification of concrete needs, grounded in real contexts and experiences, showing why these needs require a structured European response.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Contextual background
Briefly describe the context in which the needs emerge

Specify:

sector(s)

target group(s)

geographic or organisational scope

✔ Set the scene without being generic.

2. Core needs identified
Clearly list the main needs the project addresses

Needs should be:

specific

observable

shared across partners or contexts

Prioritise quality over quantity

✔ Avoid abstract needs such as “more skills” without explanation.

3. Evidence and sources
Explain how these needs were identified, e.g.:

partners’ professional experience

previous projects

feedback from target groups

observed gaps in practice

Avoid claiming needs without grounding

✔ Evaluators value credibility over statistics.

4. Needs at European level
Explain how the needs:

appear in multiple countries

vary slightly across contexts but share a common core

Show why transnational cooperation is relevant to address them

✔ This links directly to European added value.

5. Consequences if needs are not addressed
Briefly explain:

what limitations persist

what opportunities are lost

who is negatively affected

✔ This reinforces urgency and relevance.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Overly broad or generic needs

Needs disconnected from project objectives

Lack of evidence or rationale

Confusing needs with planned solutions


What Increases the Score
Clear prioritisation of needs

Strong link between needs and project logic

Grounded, practice-based analysis

Explicit European relevance
```

Full content [T43R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
 Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and appropriateness of the target groups

consistency between target groups, objectives and activities

credibility of partners’ access to and experience with the target groups

effectiveness of engagement mechanisms


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear identification of the project’s target groups, combined with a credible explanation of how partners already work with them and will actively involve them throughout the project.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Primary target groups
Clearly define the main beneficiaries

Specify:

profile

role in the project (users, co-creators, multipliers)

Avoid vague definitions

✔ Precision signals strong project grounding.

2. Secondary target groups
Identify indirect beneficiaries or multipliers

Explain their relevance to:

impact

dissemination

sustainability

✔ Keep this section focused and proportional.

3. Partners’ existing engagement with target groups
Explain how partners:

already work with these groups

have established channels, networks or practices

Refer to:

ongoing activities

previous projects

institutional roles

✔ This reassures evaluators about feasibility.

4. Engagement within the project
Describe how target groups will be involved, e.g.:

consultation

co-creation

testing or piloting

feedback and validation

Clarify when and how, not just that

✔ Active involvement scores higher than passive participation.

5. Inclusiveness and accessibility (where relevant)
Briefly mention:

how diverse profiles are reached

any specific measures to ensure access

Keep it concrete and realistic

✔ This supports inclusion without overclaiming.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Listing target groups without roles

Assuming access without explaining it

Confusing target groups with stakeholders

Overloading the project with too many audiences


What Increases the Score
Clear hierarchy of target groups

Strong link between partners and beneficiaries

Credible, practice-based engagement methods

Target groups positioned as active contributors
```

```text
Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
 Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
soundness and credibility of the needs identification process

involvement of partners and target groups in identifying needs

use of evidence, experience and reflection

coherence between identified needs and the project proposal


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and credible description of how needs were identified, showing that they are based on systematic reflection, real practice and stakeholder input, not assumptions.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Partnership-level needs
Explain how the partnership identified its own needs, e.g.:

internal reflection among partners

analysis of strengths and weaknesses

lessons learned from previous projects

Show that needs are shared across organisations

✔ This demonstrates strategic alignment.

2. Target group needs
Describe how partners identified needs of target groups through:

direct work and ongoing activities

feedback, observation or informal consultation

experience from previous initiatives

Avoid presenting needs as theoretical

✔ Evaluators value proximity to beneficiaries.

3. Evidence and sources used
Mention concrete sources, such as:

internal reports or evaluations

project reviews

structured partner discussions

Statistics are optional; credibility is key

✔ Keep it realistic and proportionate.

4. Comparative and transnational perspective
Explain how partners:

compared needs across countries

identified common patterns and differences

Show how this comparison shaped the project focus

✔ This reinforces European added value.

5. Validation of needs
Explain how needs were:

cross-checked

prioritised

validated within the partnership

Link this process to project objectives

✔ This shows intentional design, not coincidence.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Claiming needs without explaining the process

Over-reliance on generic policy statements

Confusing needs with solutions

Ignoring differences between partner contexts


What Increases the Score
Transparent and logical needs identification process

Direct involvement of partners and beneficiaries

Evidence-based reasoning grounded in practice

Clear link between needs analysis and project design
```

| Componente | Massimo suggerito |
|---|---:|
| Logic needs→objectives→activities→results | 380 |
| Map each need to the relevant choices | 380 |
| Methods and reason for their suitability | 380 |
| Usable solutions and concrete results | 380 |
| Adaptation to contexts and accessibility | 380 |
| Reserve for fittings and overhaul | 100 |
| Maximum total in template | 2000 |

### Critica e verifica richiesta

Does every activity have a causal function and verification? Eliminate disconnected actions and generic solutions.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T45R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
 Quality of the project design and implementation
 Relevance of the project
The evaluators assess in particular:
coherence between identified needs, objectives and activities

appropriateness of the proposed approach to address the needs

clarity of the intervention logic

feasibility and relevance of the proposed solutions


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear explanation of how the project’s activities, methods and outputs directly respond to the identified needs, following a coherent and realistic intervention logic.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Overall intervention logic
Briefly explain how the project is designed to respond to the needs

Show the logic:

needs → objectives → activities → results

✔ This reassures evaluators that the project is intentional and structured.

2. Mapping needs to objectives
Explicitly explain:

which needs are addressed by which objectives

Avoid one-size-fits-all solutions

✔ Clear mapping increases perceived coherence.

3. Key activities and approaches
Describe the main types of activities used to address the needs, such as:

co-creation and collaboration

capacity building

testing and piloting

development of practical tools or frameworks

Focus on why these approaches are suitable

✔ Methods matter as much as activities.

4. Outputs and practical solutions
Explain what concrete outputs will be produced to meet the needs

Show how outputs:

are usable by target groups

respond directly to identified gaps

✔ Evaluators look for solutions, not intentions.

5. Adaptation and inclusiveness
Briefly explain how the project:

adapts to different contexts

remains accessible to target groups

Keep this realistic and linked to needs

✔ This shows responsiveness, not rigidity.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Repeating objectives without explaining “how”

Listing activities without linking them to needs

Proposing generic solutions

Overloading the project with unrelated actions


What Increases the Score
Clear and logical intervention pathway

Strong link between needs, activities and outputs

Approaches grounded in practice

Solutions tailored to real gaps
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

Association to question: **editorial interpretation**, not official map or separate score. For Partnership formation and complementarity, the elements 'How the partners were actually chosen; Distinct capability of each partner; Coverage of needs and balancing of responsibilities; Results enabled by collaboration; Associated partners: concrete role and unfunded contribution' document European added value necessary for the results. It is a publishing association: there is no official question-score map in the template.

Attributions and adjacent steps of the template, preserved as non-normative content:

- [P304]: The transnational dimension clearly adds value in terms of project outcomes; the participating organisations will be able to achieve results that would not be reached by organisations from a single country.
- [P305]: Taking into account the nature of the project and its expected impact, the participating organisations have the skills and competences required to ensure that the work programme can be implemented efficiently, effectively and professionally.
- [P306]: If it is necessary for the project's success to use the expertise of organisations from different fields, and/or the project intends to impact more than one field of education, training and youth, relevant organisations of all concerned fields participate in the project.
- [P307]: The proposal demonstrates convincingly why the participation of the organisations from different fields of education, training, youth and/or other socio-economic sectors is best suited to produce the outputs that respond to the identified needs.
- [P308]: The proposal includes one or more participating organisations that are newcomers to this action and on which the impact expected from participation in the project would be particularly high.
- [P309]: The participation of organizations from Partner Countries provides genuine added value to the project because of the specific skills, experiences or expertise that these organizations bring to the project and that prove to be essential for the achievement of the project's objectives and/or to ensure a significantly higher quality of the project outputs ((if this condition is not fulfilled, the participating organization from a Partner Country will be excluded from the project proposal at assessment stage).
- [T46R1C1]: Primary criterion: /  Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements /  Quality of the project design and implementation

### Summary structure of the answer / elements to prepare

1. How the partners were actually chosen
2. Distinct capability of each partner
3. Covering needs and balancing responsibilities
4. Results enabled by collaboration
5. Associated partners: concrete role and unfunded contribution

The structure is an editorial checklist: it does not add eligibility requirements. Adapt to the relevant partner/WP/business instance.

### Critica e verifica richiesta

Are strategic choice and complementarity proven? Do not copy the previous cooperation or open invitation story without data.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T46R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Forming the Partnership:
The path of our partnership began with shared goals, joint projects and mutual respect. 
…
The other partners were selected with open invitation according to the needed skills and experience for this project. This relationship fostered trust and demonstrated successful teamwork.

Participating organisations:
P1: 

P2: 

P3: 

P4: 

P5:

Added value from their collaboration:


Associated partners:


The partnership's transnational and cross-sectoral approach brings a diverse mix of perspectives, practices, and expertise, fostering innovative solutions for…


Primary criterion:
 Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements
 Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
rationale and transparency of partnership formation

complementarity of competences and roles

balance and coherence of the partnership

added value generated through collaboration

relevance and clarity of the role of associated partners (if any)


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear explanation of how and why the partnership was formed, showing that each organisation brings distinct, complementary expertise and that collaboration is essential to achieve the project’s objectives.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Partnership formation
Explain how the partnership emerged, e.g.:

previous cooperation

existing networks

shared challenges or objectives

Show that partners were selected strategically, not opportunistically

✔ This signals intentional project design.

2. Complementarity of organisations
For each partner, briefly highlight:

core expertise

relevant experience

added value to the project

Focus on differences, not similarities

✔ Avoid generic partner descriptions.

3. Internal balance and roles
Explain how the mix of organisations ensures:

balance of perspectives

distribution of responsibilities

coverage of all project needs

Do not repeat the full WP structure

✔ Show functional logic, not bureaucracy.

4. Added value of collaboration
Explain what the partnership enables that:

a single organisation could not achieve

a less diverse partnership would miss

Emphasise:

co-creation

mutual learning

cross-context validation

✔ Collaboration must be necessary, not symbolic.

5. Associated partners (if applicable)
List associated partners and describe:

their profile

their role in the project

the added value they bring

Clarify that they:

do not receive funding

support specific activities or impact

✔ Keep this concise and concrete.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Describing partners without linking them to the project

Overlapping competences without justification

Treating associated partners as “extras”

Presenting a partnership based only on friendship


What Increases the Score
Clear logic behind partner selection

Strong complementarity of skills and experience

Well-justified added value of collaboration

Credible and balanced partnership structure
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

- [P315]: There is a clear and commonly agreed definition and an appropriate distribution of roles and tasks and a balanced participation and input of the participating organisations in the implementation of the work programme, taking into account the complementary competencies, the nature of the activities and the know-how of the partners involved.

- [P322]: in case of inclusion projects, the capacity and expertise of the consortium to support (where needed) the participation of staff or learners with special needs or fewer opportunities.

```text
Associated partners significantly support project activities with expertise and feedback on developed outcomes, pilot testing, and dissemination efforts. Their willingness to offer expert staff and resources demonstrates a strong commitment to the success of the project, increasing its ability to achieve its goals.


Primary criterion:
 Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements
 Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and coherence of task allocation

balance of responsibilities and workload among partners

level of active involvement of all participating organisations

consistency between partners’ roles and their expertise

meaningful contribution of associated partners (if any)


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and balanced allocation of tasks, showing that all partners (and associated partners, where relevant) have active, meaningful responsibilities aligned with their expertise and level of commitment.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Overall task allocation logic
Briefly explain how tasks are distributed

Show that allocation is based on:

expertise

experience

capacity

Avoid listing tasks without rationale

✔ Evaluators look for logic, not just structure.

2. Role of each partner
For each participating organisation, describe:

main responsibilities

type of contribution (coordination, development, testing, dissemination, etc.)

Keep descriptions concise and differentiated

✔ Avoid identical roles for multiple partners.

3. Balance and active contribution
Explain how the project ensures:

balanced workload

active participation throughout the project

Mention involvement across:

planning

implementation

validation

dissemination

✔ This addresses common evaluator concerns.

4. Role of associated partners (if applicable)
Describe clearly:

which tasks they support

when and how they are involved

Emphasise their practical contribution, not governance

✔ Associated partners must add value, not complexity.

5. Coordination and cooperation mechanisms
Briefly mention how partners:

coordinate tasks

collaborate across work packages

ensure quality and alignment

✔ This reinforces feasibility and cooperation quality.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
One partner doing “everything important”

Vague task descriptions

Overloading coordination partner

Associated partners listed without concrete roles


What Increases the Score
Clear rationale for task allocation

Strong alignment between tasks and expertise

Balanced and continuous partner involvement

Credible collaboration and coordination logic
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T48R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
 Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements
 Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and effectiveness of coordination structures

adequacy of communication mechanisms

inclusion of all partners (and associated partners, where relevant)

capacity to ensure smooth implementation and decision-making


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and realistic coordination and communication system that enables efficient cooperation, shared decision-making and continuous alignment among all participating organisations.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Overall coordination structure
Describe the governance model of the project, including:

coordinating organisation

main coordination bodies (e.g. steering group, coordination team)

Clarify responsibilities without excessive detail

✔ Evaluators want clarity, not bureaucracy.

2. Communication channels and tools
Specify how partners communicate, e.g.:

regular online meetings

collaborative digital tools

shared documentation spaces

Emphasise reliability and accessibility

✔ Tools are secondary to processes.

3. Frequency and formats of communication
Explain:

how often partners meet

in which formats (online, hybrid, face-to-face)

Differentiate between:

operational coordination

strategic alignment

✔ Regularity reassures evaluators.

4. Decision-making processes
Describe:

how decisions are made

how partners are involved

how conflicts or delays are addressed

Keep it simple and credible

✔ Transparency is key.

5. Involvement of associated partners (if applicable)
Explain:

how associated partners are informed

when they are involved

how communication is adapted to their role

✔ Show inclusion without overcomplication.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Overly complex management structures

Listing tools without explaining their use

Ignoring decision-making and conflict resolution

Treating associated partners as passive recipients


What Increases the Score
Clear and functional coordination logic

Regular and structured communication

Inclusive and transparent decision-making

Alignment between coordination and task allocation
```

### Pianificazione privata

- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Profile and specific expertise of the partner
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Tasks and results that depend on your input
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Essential value compared to alternatives
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Mutual learning and contribution to the project
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Integrated role, resources and eligibility verification
- [ ] Report missing data and verify crucial sources before making commitments.
- [ ] Check consistency with application, other sections, calendar, roles and budget.

This is an operational checklist of evidence and completeness; it does not require expounding or memorizing internal reasoning.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T49R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
 Relevance of the project
 Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements
The evaluators assess in particular:
necessity of involving a third country not associated to the Programme

essential added value brought by this organisation

contribution that cannot be replaced by organisations from Programme countries

coherence between the third-country partner’s role and the project objectives


What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and convincing justification showing that the involvement of the third-country organisation is essential for achieving the project’s objectives and provides unique added value that cannot be obtained otherwise.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Specific profile of the third-country organisation
Briefly describe:

expertise

experience

context-specific knowledge

Focus on what makes this organisation unique

✔ Avoid generic partner descriptions.

2. Essential contribution to the project
Clearly explain:

which tasks or activities depend on this organisation

what would be missing or weaker without its involvement

Link contribution directly to:

objectives

results

innovation elements

✔ “Essential” means not easily replaceable.

3. Added value compared to Programme-country partners
Explain why the same contribution:

cannot be provided by partners from Programme countries

would significantly reduce quality or relevance if replaced

Emphasise uniqueness, not diversity alone

✔ Geographic diversity alone is not sufficient.

4. Contribution to European learning and perspective
Explain how this organisation:

brings alternative approaches or models

enriches comparative analysis

strengthens innovation or transferability

✔ Show two-way learning, not one-directional input.

5. Proportional and well-integrated role
Show that the organisation:

has a clearly defined and limited role

is fully integrated in the partnership

contributes actively, not symbolically

✔ Over- or under-involvement both raise red flags.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Justifying involvement only through “international perspective”

Assigning marginal or generic tasks

Vague statements about diversity or networking

Lack of explanation on why the partner is indispensable


What Increases the Score
Strong necessity-based justification

Clear link between the partner’s role and project success

Demonstrated non-substitutability

Credible integration into the partnership and workflow
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T50R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
robustness of the monitoring system
clarity of roles and responsibilities in monitoring
adequacy of timing and frequency of monitoring activities
capacity to ensure quality control and corrective action

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear, structured and realistic monitoring system that tracks progress, quality and results throughout the project, with defined responsibilities and regular review moments.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Overall monitoring framework
Describe the monitoring approach used to oversee:
progress of activities
quality of outputs
achievement of objectives
Show that monitoring is continuous, not only final
✔ Evaluators look for an embedded process, not a one-off check.

2. Roles and involved staff
Clearly indicate:
who is responsible for monitoring (e.g. coordinator, WP leaders, project team)
how partners contribute to monitoring
Highlight:
internal quality checks
shared responsibility across the partnership
✔ Avoid concentrating all monitoring on one person.

3. Monitoring tools and methods
Describe the main tools used, such as:
work plans and timelines
progress reports
quality checklists
partner feedback or review moments
Keep tools proportionate to project size
✔ Practical tools score higher than abstract frameworks.

4. Timing and frequency
Specify:
how often monitoring takes place (e.g. monthly, per milestone, per WP)
when key review moments occur
Distinguish between:
operational monitoring
strategic or milestone-based review
✔ Regularity increases credibility.

5. Use of monitoring results
Explain how monitoring outcomes are used to:
adjust activities
address delays or risks
improve quality
Mention corrective measures where relevant
✔ Monitoring must inform decisions.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Vague statements like “the project will be monitored regularly”
No link between monitoring and decision-making
Lack of clarity on responsibilities
Monitoring described only at reporting stage

What Increases the Score
Clear and simple monitoring logic
Defined roles and shared responsibility
Regular and structured review moments
Monitoring clearly linked to quality improvement
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

```text
Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation
Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements
The evaluators assess in particular:
robustness of financial management and budget control
coherence between budget, activities and timeline
clarity of roles and responsibilities for financial and time management
capacity to prevent delays and financial risks

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and realistic system for budget control and time management, showing that resources and timelines are actively monitored and adjusted to ensure smooth project implementation.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Budget control framework
Describe how the project budget is:
planned
monitored
reviewed
Emphasise transparency and compliance with Erasmus+ rules
✔ Evaluators want control, not complexity.

2. Roles and responsibilities
Clearly indicate:
who is responsible for financial management
how partners report costs and progress
Distinguish between:
coordination-level control
partner-level responsibility
✔ Clear roles reduce perceived risk.

3. Financial monitoring tools and processes
Describe practical tools, such as:
budget tracking tables
periodic financial reviews
internal checks against planned activities
Avoid unnecessary technical detail
✔ Simplicity increases credibility.

4. Time management and scheduling
Explain how the project timeline is:
structured (work plan, milestones)
monitored regularly
updated if needed
Mention alignment between:
activities
deliverables
reporting moments
✔ Time and budget should be clearly linked.

5. Risk prevention and corrective measures
Briefly describe:
how delays or budget deviations are detected early
how corrective actions are decided and implemented
Keep this realistic and proportionate
✔ Shows readiness, not pessimism.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Generic statements about “good management”
Overly rigid planning with no flexibility
No link between financial control and activities
Ignoring partner-level responsibilities

What Increases the Score
Clear and transparent control mechanisms
Alignment between budget, tasks and timeline
Shared responsibility across partners
Proactive approach to risks and adjustments
```

Full content [T53R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Relevance of the project
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
integration of inclusion and accessibility by design
adequacy of measures addressing diverse needs
coherence between target groups, activities and inclusion measures
realism and feasibility of proposed actions

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and concrete explanation of how inclusion and accessibility are embedded in the project design, ensuring that activities are genuinely accessible to participants with diverse needs.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Inclusion and accessibility as a design principle
Explain that accessibility and inclusion are:
considered from the planning phase
integrated across all activities
Avoid presenting them as add-ons
✔ “By design” is a key evaluator concept.

2. Identification of barriers
Briefly describe:
potential barriers (physical, digital, linguistic, socio-economic, cultural)
Show awareness of different types of obstacles
✔ Evaluators value anticipation of challenges.

3. Inclusive design of activities
Explain how activities are:
flexible in format and delivery
adaptable to different learning needs
designed to encourage active participation
Refer to methods where relevant
✔ Methodology matters more than declarations.

4. Accessibility measures and support
Describe concrete measures, such as:
accessible venues and digital tools
clear and inclusive communication
support mechanisms for participants with fewer opportunities
Keep measures realistic and proportional
✔ Avoid listing measures unrelated to your activities.

5. Continuous monitoring and adaptation
Explain how inclusion and accessibility are:
monitored during implementation
adjusted based on feedback
Link this to overall quality monitoring
✔ Shows responsiveness, not rigidity.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Generic references to inclusion without measures
Treating accessibility as only physical access
Overpromising support without capacity
Copy-pasting Programme Guide language

What Increases the Score
Inclusion embedded from the start
Clear understanding of diverse barriers
Practical and adaptable measures
Ongoing attention throughout the project
```

3. Collaborazione, co-creazione e documentazione
4. Quality of learning and digital accessibility
5. Reuse and Erasmus+ platforms relevant to the sector

The structure is an editorial checklist: it does not add eligibility requirements. Adapt to the relevant partner/WP/business instance.

### Pianificazione privata

- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Function of digital tools and methods
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Integration between online preparation and physical activities
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Collaboration, co-creation and documentation
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Learning quality and digital accessibility
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Reuse and Erasmus+ platforms relevant to the sector
- [ ] Report missing data and verify crucial sources before making commitments.
- [ ] Check consistency with application, other sections, calendar, roles and budget.

This is an operational checklist of evidence and completeness; it does not require expounding or memorizing internal reasoning.

| Componente | Massimo suggerito |
|---|---:|
| Funzione di strumenti e metodi digitali | 570 |

### Critica e verifica richiesta

Does each tool have a function and benefit? Don't assume that a digital tool will make the project innovative.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T54R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Relevance of the project
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
strategic and meaningful use of digital tools and methods
coherence between digital and physical activities
contribution of digital solutions to cooperation and coordination
added value of digital approaches for learning quality and efficiency

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear explanation of how digital tools and learning methods are purposefully integrated to complement physical activities, strengthen cooperation, and improve the quality and efficiency of project implementation.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Role of digital tools in the project
Explain why digital tools are used in the project
Position them as:
complementary to physical activities
enabling continuity and flexibility
✔ Digital is a means, not an end.

2. Digital tools for cooperation and coordination
Describe how partners use digital tools to:
coordinate tasks
share documents and knowledge
communicate regularly
Emphasise reliability and shared access
✔ Focus on cooperation, not platforms.

3. Digital learning methods
Explain how digital tools support learning through:
collaborative work
blended or hybrid formats
peer exchange and feedback
Highlight learner-centred approaches
✔ Methods matter more than technology.

4. Complementarity with physical activities
Show how digital activities:
prepare or follow up physical meetings
extend learning beyond face-to-face moments
increase participation and accessibility
✔ Evaluators value integration, not separation.

5. Added value and sustainability
Explain how digital solutions:
improve efficiency
reduce barriers
can be reused after the project
✔ This links digitalisation to impact.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Listing tools without explaining their function
Overemphasising platforms instead of learning design
Treating digital and physical activities as separate
Claiming innovation without added value

What Increases the Score
Clear rationale for digital choices
Strong integration of digital and physical elements
Digital tools supporting cooperation and learning
Sustainable and reusable digital approaches
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T55R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Relevance of the project
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
integration of green practices across project phases
coherence between environmental measures and project activities
realism and feasibility of proposed actions
contribution to environmental awareness and responsibility

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear explanation of how environmental sustainability is embedded throughout the project lifecycle, with concrete and realistic green practices linked to planning, implementation and follow-up.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Green approach as a guiding principle
Explain that green practices are:
integrated from the planning phase
considered in all major project decisions
Avoid presenting sustainability as an add-on
✔ Evaluators look for consistency, not slogans.

2. Green practices in project planning
Describe how environmental considerations influence:
choice of activities and formats
use of digital solutions where appropriate
reduction of unnecessary travel or materials
✔ Planning-level choices matter most.

3. Green practices during implementation
Explain concrete measures adopted during activities, such as:
paperless workflows
environmentally responsible venues and logistics
promotion of sustainable travel options
Keep measures proportionate and realistic
✔ Avoid listing actions unrelated to your activities.

4. Green practices in cooperation and dissemination
Describe how green principles guide:
partner cooperation
communication and dissemination activities
Emphasise low-impact and digital-first approaches where relevant
✔ Sustainability should also shape communication.

5. Awareness and long-term effects
Explain how the project:
promotes environmentally responsible behaviour
embeds green awareness in methods or outputs
Focus on behavioural and organisational change
✔ Long-term mindset change scores higher than one-off actions.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Generic references to “being sustainable”
Overpromising environmental impact
Isolated green actions with no coherence
Copying Programme Guide language

What Increases the Score
Green practices embedded across phases
Clear link between sustainability and project design
Realistic and feasible measures
Contribution to long-term environmental awareness
```

Full content [T56R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Relevance of the project
Impact
The evaluators assess in particular:
integration of participation and civic engagement by design
active involvement of target groups across different project phases
contribution to democratic life, EU values and active citizenship
credibility of participation mechanisms beyond formal consultation

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear explanation of how participants and stakeholders are actively involved throughout the project lifecycle, contributing to decision-making, co-creation and civic engagement in a meaningful way.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Participation as a guiding principle
Explain that participation and civic engagement are:
embedded from the project design phase
not limited to dissemination or final events
Frame participants as active contributors, not beneficiaries only
✔ Evaluators look for participation “by design”.

2. Participation in the design and preparation phase
Describe how target groups:
contribute to defining needs, priorities or approaches
provide input through consultation, reflection or co-design
Keep it realistic and proportionate
✔ Early involvement increases credibility.

3. Participation during implementation
Explain how participants are:
involved in activities as co-creators, testers or facilitators
encouraged to express views, collaborate and make decisions
Highlight participatory and non-formal methods where relevant
✔ Active roles score higher than passive attendance.

4. Civic engagement and democratic dimension
Explain how the project:
strengthens critical thinking, dialogue or civic awareness
encourages engagement with community, society or EU values
Link engagement to concrete actions or outputs
✔ Civic engagement should be practical, not rhetorical.

5. Participation in evaluation, dissemination and follow-up
Describe how participants:
contribute feedback and reflection
help share results
remain engaged beyond the project duration
✔ This reinforces impact and sustainability.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Treating participation as attendance only
Limiting civic engagement to awareness-raising
Involving participants only at the end
Overgeneralising democratic impact

What Increases the Score
Participation integrated across all phases
Clear and active roles for target groups
Concrete civic engagement mechanisms
Strong link between participation and impact
```

### Critica e verifica richiesta

CONFLICT: T57 denies a cap and suggests over 20%; Program p243 and expert p49 prescribe a maximum of 20%. Do not use bands beyond 20%.

Note di curatela:

- The proportions 10–20%,20–25% and 25–30% are unvalidated template opinions; the former do not constitute an official minimum.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T57R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Formal limits
No explicit percentage cap is defined in the Programme Guide.

However, WP1 is assessed under proportionality and necessity.

If WP1 is too high, evaluators can:

reduce the score

consider it poor value for money

flag imbalance in the partnership

➡️ So the limit is implicit, not numerical.

Strong evaluator-backed best practices
1. Typical proportion (rule of thumb)
While not official, good-quality KA220 projects usually allocate:
10–20% of the total project budget to WP1

Rarely above 20–25%, unless:

many partners (7–10)

high coordination complexity

strong compliance/reporting load

❌ >25–30% = high-risk zone unless extremely well justified.

2. WP1 must cover only management
WP1 budget should clearly correspond to:
coordination & governance

financial and administrative management

monitoring & quality assurance

reporting to the National Agency

❌ Red flag:
WP1 used to “absorb” under-planned content work

WP1 larger than core content WPs


3. Proportionality to project structure
Evaluators check coherence between:
WP1 budget

number of partners

number of WPs

project duration

✔ Examples:
3 partners / 24 months → lean WP1

8 partners / 36 months → stronger WP1 justified


4. Balance across partners
Best practice:
Coordinator receives the largest share, but

Other partners still have recognisable WP1 effort

❌ Red flag:
90–100% of WP1 on the coordinator

Other partners described as “active” but not budgeted


5. Narrative consistency (crucial)
WP1 budget must match exactly what you describe in:
coordination mechanisms

monitoring & QA

financial control

decision-making processes

✔ If WP1 is high → governance must look solid and demanding
 ✔ If WP1 is low → management must look lean and efficient

What evaluators like to see
WP1 is not the biggest WP

Clear separation between:

management (WP1)

content / outputs (WP2–WPx)

Budget reflects real coordination work, not comfort margin


Quick self-check (expert-style)
Ask yourself:
Could this project realistically be managed with this WP1 budget?

Does WP1 look supportive of the project, not dominant?

Is WP1 consistent with how “complex” we claim the project is?

If yes → you’re safe.
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T58R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Specific objectives:
Project objectives should be Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant and Time-bound (i.e. 'S.M.A.R.T')
show a clear correlation between objectives, activities carried out and results achieved


Contribution to the General Objectives:

Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and relevance of WP-specific objectives
coherence between WP objectives and overall project objectives
contribution of the WP to project logic and expected results
internal consistency of the work package

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and focused set of work package objectives that directly support the overall project objectives and clearly define the role of this WP within the project logic.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Purpose of the work package
Briefly explain what this WP is about
Position it within the project:
foundational
developmental
implementation
validation
✔ This helps evaluators “place” the WP.

2. WP-specific objectives
Define 2–4 specific objectives for the WP
Each objective should be:
concrete
action-oriented
clearly linked to WP tasks
Use formulations like:
to develop
to design
to test
to validate
✔ Avoid vague or overarching objectives.

3. Contribution to overall project objectives
Explicitly explain:
how each WP objective contributes to one or more project objectives
Show logical dependency (e.g. WP2 enables WP3/WP4)
✔ Evaluators want to see vertical coherence.

4. Added value of the WP
Briefly explain:
why this WP is necessary
what would be missing without it
Keep this concise and functional
✔ This strengthens internal logic.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Repeating project objectives verbatim
Mixing objectives with activities
Overloading the WP with too many objectives
Weak or implicit link to project-level objectives

What Increases the Score
Clear distinction between WP and project objectives
Strong contribution logic
Focused and manageable number of objectives
WP positioned clearly in the overall project structure
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T59R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Within this WP, we will develop …

Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and concreteness of WP results
coherence between WP objectives, activities and results
relevance and usability of results
contribution of WP results to project outcomes

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear description of the concrete and tangible results produced by the work package, showing how they directly stem from its objectives and activities and contribute to the overall project results.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Main results overview
Clearly list the key results of the WP
Results should be:
concrete
identifiable
achievable within the WP timeframe
✔ Think in terms of “what exists at the end of the WP”.

2. Link to WP objectives
Briefly explain how each result:
responds to one or more WP objectives
Avoid generic outcomes not anchored to objectives
✔ One result should never feel disconnected.

3. Nature of the results
Clarify whether results are:
intellectual outputs (frameworks, tools, curricula, models)
tested or validated practices
processes or systems
Emphasise usability and applicability
✔ Evaluators value results that can be used beyond the project.

4. Target users of the results
Indicate who will:
use the results
benefit from them
Link results to project target groups
✔ This prepares impact and dissemination.

5. Role of this WP within the project flow
Explain how results:
feed into subsequent WPs
enable further activities or outputs
✔ Shows horizontal and vertical coherence.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Describing activities instead of results
Listing abstract outcomes (e.g. “improved skills”) without outputs
Overlapping results with other WPs
Missing link to project logic

What Increases the Score
Clear, tangible results
Strong link to WP objectives and project flow
Results designed for use and transfer
Well-positioned within the overall project structure
```
