### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T60R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Several indicators have been settled to measure the achievement of the WP objectives during its implementation.

Quantitative indicators:


Qualitative indicators:


Inclusion indicators:

Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
appropriateness of qualitative and quantitative indicators
coherence between WP objectives, results and indicators
capacity of indicators to measure both achievement and quality
feasibility and usefulness of the monitoring approach

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and balanced set of qualitative and quantitative indicators that realistically measure both the achievement of the WP objectives and the quality of the results produced.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Indicator framework overview
Briefly explain that the WP uses:
quantitative indicators to measure outputs and reach
qualitative indicators to assess quality, relevance and usability
Emphasise proportionality
✔ Evaluators look for balance, not complexity.

2. Quantitative indicators (achievement)
Define indicators that measure:
completion of planned results
participation or involvement (where relevant)
milestones or deliverables achieved
Indicators should be:
concrete
measurable
directly linked to WP objectives
✔ Avoid vanity metrics.

3. Qualitative indicators (quality)
Define indicators that assess:
relevance and usefulness of results
coherence and clarity
applicability in real contexts
Use sources such as:
partner peer review
target group feedback
testing or validation sessions
✔ Quality must be explicitly measured, not assumed.

4. Data sources and collection
Briefly indicate:
how data will be collected
who is involved in assessment
Keep it realistic and light
✔ Evaluators want credible processes.

5. Use of indicators
Explain how indicator results will be used to:
improve outputs
adjust WP activities if needed
validate readiness for next WPs
✔ Indicators must inform decisions.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Indicators not linked to objectives
Only quantitative indicators
Indicators that measure activities instead of results
Overly complex or unrealistic measurement systems

What Increases the Score
Clear link between objectives, results and indicators
Balanced qualitative and quantitative approach
Indicators focused on usefulness and quality
Practical and feasible monitoring logic
```

Attributions and adjacent steps of the template, preserved as non-normative content:

- [P401]: There is a clear and commonly agreed definition and an appropriate distribution of roles and tasks and a balanced participation and input of the participating organisations in the implementation of the work programme, taking into account the complementary competencies, the nature of the activities and the know-how of the partners involved.
- [P402]: The proposal concretely identifies which skills, experiences, expertise and management support each of the participating organisations will make available to implement all aspects of the project proposed.
- [P403]: The proposal shows that the participating organisations have established and will run a cohesive consortium with active involvement of all partners and with common goals to be achieved.
- [P404]: In this respect, the following factors should be taken into consideration during the assessment:
- [P405]: the level of networking, cooperation and commitment of each participating organisation in the project;
- [P406]: the profile and background of participating organisations when nature or target of the activity would necessitate the possession of certain qualifications;
- [P407]: the capacity of the consortium to ensure effective implementation, follow-up and dissemination of the results achieved through the project.
- [P408]: in case of inclusion projects, the capacity and expertise of the consortium to support (where needed) the participation of staff or learners with special needs or fewer opportunities.
- [T61R1C1]: Primary criterion: / Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements / Quality of the project design and implementation

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T61R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
All partners will be actively involved in the activities, bringing their skills and knowledge to produce high-quality materials. However, the WP will have a lead partner to ensure the proper division of tasks and better involvement of all partners in creating the output.

Primary criterion:
Quality of the partnership and cooperation arrangements
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity of task distribution within the WP
coherence between partners’ roles and expertise
balance of responsibilities and workload
level of active contribution by all partners involved in the WP

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and differentiated description of each partner’s tasks and responsibilities within the work package, showing how their expertise is used and how their contribution is essential to achieving the WP objectives.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. WP leadership and coordination
Identify the WP leader
Describe responsibilities such as:
coordinating WP activities
ensuring alignment with objectives and timeline
overseeing quality and delivery
✔ Keep this focused on coordination, not content ownership.

2. Tasks of each partner organisation
For each partner involved in the WP, briefly describe:
main tasks (development, contribution, testing, validation, dissemination within WP)
type of contribution (technical, methodological, contextual, operational)
link between tasks and the partner’s specific expertise
✔ Use differentiated roles; avoid copy-paste descriptions.

3. Collaboration and interdependence
Explain how partners:
work together on shared tasks
contribute to common outputs
review or validate each other’s work
✔ Evaluators look for cooperation, not parallel work.

4. Proportionality of involvement
Show that:
workload is balanced
responsibilities are realistic
no partner has a purely marginal role
✔ Balance reinforces partnership quality.

5. Contribution of associated partners (if applicable)
Describe:
which tasks they support
at which stage of the WP
Focus on practical input, not governance
✔ Their role must be meaningful and clearly delimited.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Generic descriptions for all partners
Tasks not linked to WP objectives
One partner doing most of the substantive work
Associated partners listed without concrete responsibilities

What Increases the Score
Clear and differentiated partner roles
Strong alignment between tasks and expertise
Evidence of real collaboration
Balanced and credible workload distribution
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T62R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation
Impact
The evaluators assess in particular:
coherence between WP budget and planned tasks
justification of the amount allocated to the WP
cost-effectiveness and value for money
proportionality of resources in relation to expected results

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and transparent explanation of how the WP budget was calculated, demonstrating that resources are proportionate, necessary and efficiently used to achieve the WP objectives and results.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Budget definition logic
Explain how the WP budget was defined, based on:
scope and complexity of WP objectives
number and type of tasks
duration of the WP
number of partners involved
Emphasise needs-based planning, not fixed allocations
✔ Evaluators look for rationale, not formulas.

2. Link between tasks and costs
Describe how the budget reflects:
specific tasks and responsibilities
level of effort required from partners
Show that:
each major cost line corresponds to concrete activities
no costs are disconnected from WP tasks
✔ Strong task–cost coherence is essential.

3. Proportionality and balance
Explain why the allocated amount is:
proportionate to the WP’s role in the project
consistent with other WPs
Highlight balance between:
coordination
development
validation or testing activities
✔ Avoid WP budgets that look inflated or marginal.

4. Cost-effectiveness measures
Explain how the WP ensures efficient use of resources, e.g.:
shared responsibilities among partners
reuse of existing competences and tools
avoidance of duplication
combination of online and physical work where appropriate
✔ Cost-effectiveness is about how money is used, not only how much.

5. Value for money and expected return
Explain how the WP budget leads to:
high-quality results
outputs usable beyond the project
benefits for multiple target groups
Emphasise sustainability and transferability
✔ Evaluators assess return on investment.

6. Monitoring and adjustment
Briefly describe how WP costs are:
monitored during implementation
adjusted if necessary to maintain efficiency
Link this to overall financial control mechanisms
✔ Shows responsible financial management.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Vague statements like “the budget is appropriate”
No explanation of how figures were calculated
WP budget disconnected from tasks
Claiming cost-effectiveness without examples

What Increases the Score
Clear needs-based budget rationale
Strong link between tasks, effort and costs
Demonstrated efficiency and reuse of resources
Convincing value-for-money narrative
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T64R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
All activities in WP2 are designed to ensure inclusivity and accessibility, aligned with the Erasmus+ Inclusion and Diversity Strategy. The WP aims to …


Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and coherence of WP activities
relevance of activities in relation to WP objectives
logical sequencing and feasibility of activities
contribution of activities to concrete results

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and structured description of the WP activities, explaining what will be done, in which order and why, and how these activities lead to the expected results.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Overall activity logic
Briefly explain the purpose of the activity set
Describe how activities are:
logically sequenced
mutually reinforcing
Avoid listing disconnected actions
✔ Evaluators want to see a process, not a checklist.

2. Description of main activities
Describe each core activity, focusing on:
content
approach or methodology
expected contribution to WP results
Use clear and concise descriptions
✔ Explain what happens during the activity, not just its title.

3. Methodological approach
Explain how activities are carried out, e.g.:
collaborative work
co-creation
testing and iteration
Show alignment with WP objectives
✔ Method choice signals quality.

4. Involvement of partners and target groups
Briefly explain:
which partners are involved
how target groups contribute (if applicable)
Keep it proportional
✔ Reinforces feasibility and relevance.

5. Link to outputs and next WPs
Explain how activities:
directly produce WP results
feed into subsequent WPs
Keep this functional and explicit
✔ Shows coherence across the project.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Repeating objectives instead of describing activities
Overly technical or vague descriptions
Activities not clearly linked to results
Overloading the WP with too many actions

What Increases the Score
Clear sequencing of activities
Strong link between activities and results
Coherent and realistic methodology
Activities designed to produce usable outputs
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T65R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
The primary goal of WP… is to …

Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
coherence between activities and WP objectives
clarity of the intervention logic at WP level
appropriateness of activities to achieve intended results
feasibility of the proposed approach

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear explanation of how each activity directly contributes to achieving the WP objectives, following a logical and coherent cause–effect pathway.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. WP-level intervention logic
Briefly restate the core purpose of the WP
Explain the logic:
activities → results → WP objectives
✔ Evaluators want to see intentional design.

2. Mapping activities to WP objectives
Explicitly explain:
which activities support which WP objectives
Avoid generic statements like “all activities contribute to all objectives”
✔ Clear mapping strengthens coherence.

3. Contribution of each activity
For each main activity, explain:
what it delivers
how this output advances a specific WP objective
Keep explanations focused and concrete
✔ Show cause–effect, not intention.

4. Synergy between activities
Explain how activities:
build on each other
reinforce outcomes when combined
Highlight sequencing where relevant
✔ Shows that the WP is more than the sum of parts.

5. Readiness for next phases
Briefly explain how achieving WP objectives:
enables subsequent WPs
supports overall project objectives
✔ Reinforces vertical coherence.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Repeating activity descriptions without explanation
Vague claims of contribution
Lack of explicit links to objectives
Treating activities as independent actions

What Increases the Score
Clear activity–objective mapping
Strong causal logic
Well-sequenced activities
Coherent WP narrative
```

Full content [T66R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
We expect the following results from the activities in this WP:

Tangible results:



Intangible results:



Transferability potential:



Recognition of the learning outcomes at the end of the…

Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and concreteness of activity-level results
coherence between activities and their results
relevance of results for WP and project objectives
usability and transferability of results

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear description of the concrete results produced by the activities, showing how they directly stem from the activities and contribute to the WP and overall project objectives.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Overview of activity results
Briefly summarise the main results generated by the activities
Focus on what will exist or change as a direct consequence
✔ Results ≠ activities.

2. Activity-to-result linkage
Explain how:
each key activity leads to specific results
Keep the link explicit and logical
✔ Avoid generic or indirect outcomes.

3. Nature of the results
Clarify whether results are:
tangible outputs (documents, tools, frameworks)
tested or validated practices
improved processes or methods
Emphasise practical usability
✔ Evaluators value concrete deliverables.

4. Quality and readiness of results
Explain expected level of:
completeness
quality
readiness for use
Link this to quality assurance where relevant
✔ Shows seriousness about outputs.

5. Contribution to WP and project objectives
Explain briefly how activity results:
fulfil WP objectives
support overall project goals
✔ Reinforces coherence.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Re-describing activities
Listing abstract impacts instead of results
Overlapping results with other WPs
No link to objectives

What Increases the Score
Clear, tangible results
Strong activity–result linkage
Results designed for use and transfer
Coherent integration into project logic
```

```text
Participants in …

Personal Data and GDPR
Any personal data we need for the successful production and implementation of the focus group and hackathons will be securely stored according to the GDPR rules of the EU. Primary criterion:
Relevance of the project
Quality of the project design and implementation
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and relevance of the participant profile
coherence between participants, activities and objectives
adequacy of the number of participants
inclusion of diverse profiles, where relevant

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and justified description of the number and profile of participants, showing that they are appropriate for the activities and essential to achieving the WP objectives.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)

State the estimated number of participants involved in the WP activities
If numbers vary by activity, explain briefly

2. Participant profiles
Describe the key characteristics of participants, such as:
role or function (educators, youth workers, learners, staff, etc.)
level of experience or expertise
connection to the project topic
Keep profiles focused and relevant
✔ Avoid overly broad target descriptions.

3. Link to WP objectives and activities
Explain why these participants:
are needed for the WP activities
contribute to achieving WP objectives
Make the functional link explicit
✔ Participation must be purposeful.

4. Balance and diversity
Explain how the participant group ensures:
diversity of perspectives
balance between experience levels or contexts
Mention inclusion aspects only if relevant to WP2
✔ Diversity should support quality, not just compliance.

5. Role of participants in the WP
Clarify whether participants act as:
contributors
testers
validators
end users
Avoid describing them as passive beneficiaries
✔ Active roles score higher.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Inflated or unjustified participant numbers
Generic profiles disconnected from activities
Treating participants as audience only
Ignoring coherence with WP objectives

What Increases the Score
Realistic and justified participant numbers
Clearly defined and relevant profiles
Strong link between participants and WP activities
Active participant roles
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T108R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Impact
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity of the impact assessment framework
coherence between objectives, indicators and evidence
appropriateness of methods to measure achievement
capacity to demonstrate results and change

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and credible framework to assess whether the project objectives have been achieved, using appropriate indicators, evidence sources and assessment methods.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Overall impact assessment approach
Explain that objective achievement is assessed through:
a combination of quantitative and qualitative methods
continuous and final assessment moments
Emphasise proportionality
✔ Evaluators want a system, not isolated checks.

2. Mapping objectives to indicators
Explain how:
each project objective is linked to specific indicators
Mention:
output-based indicators
outcome-based indicators
✔ Direct mapping increases credibility.

3. Assessment tools and methods
Describe tools used to assess achievement, such as:
monitoring data from WPs
feedback from target groups
validation or testing results
Focus on evidence, not opinion
✔ Evaluators look for demonstrable proof.

4. Timing and responsibility
Explain:
when assessment takes place (mid-term, final, milestone-based)
who is responsible for assessment
Keep roles clear and realistic
✔ Timing matters as much as tools.

5. Use of assessment results
Explain how assessment outcomes:
confirm achievement
identify gaps
inform final adjustments or follow-up actions
✔ Assessment must inform decisions.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Vague statements like “objectives will be evaluated”
No link between objectives and indicators
Relying only on self-assessment
Assessment only at the end

What Increases the Score
Clear objective–indicator mapping
Mixed-method assessment approach
Regular and structured evaluation moments
Evidence-based achievement verification
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T109R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Impact
The evaluators assess in particular:
credibility of the sustainability strategy
long-term organisational development effects
continued use and exploitation of project results
capacity to ensure impact beyond EU funding

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and realistic sustainability strategy showing how the project will generate lasting benefits for the participating organisations and how its results will continue to be used after the project ends.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Sustainability embedded in project design
Explain that sustainability is:
considered from the start
integrated into objectives, activities and outputs
Avoid presenting sustainability as a final add-on
✔ Evaluators value “built-in” sustainability.

2. Organisational development of partners
Explain how participation in the project will:
strengthen partners’ competences
improve methods, practices or internal processes
increase capacity for future cooperation or innovation
Focus on concrete organisational change
✔ Capacity-building is a key sustainability lever.

3. Long-term use of project results
Explain how results will be:
integrated into partners’ regular activities
reused in future projects, training or services
Emphasise practicality and adaptability
✔ Outputs should not remain project-bound.

4. Continuation of activities after the project
Describe which activities:
can be replicated
can be scaled or adapted
may continue with local or other funding
Keep this realistic and proportional
✔ Sustainability ≠ permanence of everything.

5. Transferability and wider uptake
Explain how results:
can be adopted by external organisations
support broader impact at sector or community level
Link this to dissemination and exploitation logic
✔ Wider uptake strengthens sustainability.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Claiming sustainability without mechanisms
Relying only on future funding
Vague statements about “continuing cooperation”
Treating dissemination as sustainability

What Increases the Score
Clear link between project participation and organisational growth
Practical plans for continued use of results
Realistic continuation of selected activities
Strong focus on capacity-building and reuse
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T110R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Impact
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and relevance of expected impact on organisations and target groups
credibility of integration into regular work
scale and depth of organisational and practice-level change
coherence between outcomes, needs and long-term use

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A clear and credible description of the concrete impact on participating organisations and target groups, showing how outcomes will be embedded into regular practices rather than remaining project-based.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Impact on participating organisations
Describe concrete changes for organisations, such as:
strengthened competences and skills
improved methods, tools or workflows
increased capacity for cooperation and innovation
Focus on operational impact, not visibility
✔ Organisational learning is a core impact dimension.

2. Impact on target groups
Explain how target groups will:
benefit directly from the project outcomes
experience changes in practices, access, or opportunities
Keep impact specific and realistic
✔ Avoid generic statements like “participants will be empowered”.

3. Integration into regular work
Explain how outcomes will be embedded, for example:
incorporation into existing programmes or services
adoption as standard tools or methods
use in routine training, education or youth work activities
Emphasise continuity beyond the project
✔ Integration is stronger than future intention.

4. Internal dissemination and uptake
Describe how organisations will:
share outcomes internally
support staff or members in using them
Keep this practical and proportional
✔ Internal uptake is a key sustainability signal.

5. Long-term perspective
Explain how the impact:
extends beyond the project duration
contributes to long-term development of organisations and communities
Avoid overclaiming scale
✔ Credibility beats ambition.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Mixing impact with dissemination
Describing only short-term benefits
No explanation of integration into regular work
Overly abstract or inflated impact claims

What Increases the Score
Clear differentiation between organisational and target-group impact
Concrete examples of integration into daily practice
Strong link between outcomes and long-term change
Realistic and evidence-based impact narrative
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T111R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Primary criterion:
Impact
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and plausibility of wider (systemic) impact
relevance across local, regional, national and European levels
potential for uptake beyond the partnership
contribution to policy, practice and sector development

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A credible and well-argued explanation of how project results generate effects beyond the partnership, influencing practices, organisations or ecosystems at multiple territorial levels.

Recommended Answer Structure (compact)
1. Scope and levels of impact
Clearly state which levels the project targets (local, regional, national, European)
Explain why these levels are relevant for the field of application
✔ Be selective; depth beats breadth.

2. Local and regional impact
Describe how results:
strengthen local practices or services
support organisations, professionals or communities
Emphasise replicability within similar contexts
✔ Show concrete use cases.

3. National impact
Explain how results can:
inform national practices, standards or programmes
support professional development or organisational change
Avoid policy claims unless realistic and justified
✔ Focus on sector-level influence.

4. European impact
Explain how the project:
addresses shared European challenges
produces results relevant across countries
enables cross-country learning and transfer
Highlight European added value without repeating earlier sections
✔ Emphasise comparability and transferability.

5. Mechanisms enabling wider impact
Briefly describe how impact spreads, e.g.:
open-access results
integration into networks or platforms
use by multipliers or umbrella organisations
✔ Impact needs channels, not assumptions.

6. Durability of wider impact
Explain why wider impact:
can last beyond the project duration
is not dependent solely on EU funding
Keep claims realistic
✔ Sustainability reinforces impact.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Generic statements about “European relevance”
Claiming policy impact without mechanisms
Confusing dissemination reach with impact
Overstating scale without evidence

What Increases the Score
Clear differentiation of impact levels
Concrete pathways from results to wider use
Strong European relevance and transfer logic
Credible, non-inflated impact narrative
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T112R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
-Objectives, proposed approach, scope, results, impact.


Primary criterion:
Relevance of the project
Impact
The evaluators assess in particular:
clarity and relevance of the project objectives
coherence with identified needs and priorities
ambition balanced with feasibility
potential contribution to meaningful change

What evaluators expect (in one sentence)
A small set of clear, concrete and achievable objectives that directly respond to identified needs and clearly state what the project aims to change or improve.

Recommended Answer Structure (very compact – 500 chars)
1. Focused objective statement
Define 3–4 concise objectives
Use action-oriented language:
to develop
to strengthen
to improve
to enable
✔ Avoid descriptive or narrative sentences.

2. Link to needs and impact
Ensure each objective:
responds to a clearly identified need
implies a concrete result or change
Avoid explaining how (that comes later)
✔ Objectives describe what will be achieved, not activities.

3. Alignment with priorities
Ensure objectives implicitly reflect:
Erasmus+ priorities
European added value
Do not name priorities explicitly unless space allows
✔ Subtle alignment is stronger than listing.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Too many objectives
Vague or aspirational wording
Objectives that are actually activities
Repeating the project description

What Increases the Score
Precision and clarity
Action-oriented phrasing
Direct link to needs and impact
Realistic ambition within project scope
```

```text
-Activities that will bring the results/outputs

Primary criterion:
Quality of the project design and implementation

clarity and coherence of the planned activities

development or co-creation of outputs
testing or piloting
dissemination and exploitation

logically sequenced
coherent as a project cycle
Avoid unnecessary detail
✔ This is a snapshot, not a WP description.

Typical Mistakes to Avoid
Repeating objectives
Overly technical descriptions
Listing tools instead of activities
Too much detail for the space

What Increases the Score
Clear and structured activity list
Direct link to objectives
Feasible and well-ordered actions
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T115R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
What evaluators use this annex for
To verify coherence between activities, WPs and costs

To assess proportionality, realism and value for money

To reduce risk on financial management and implementation feasibility

This annex strongly reinforces:
Quality of project design & implementation

Quality of partnership & cooperation

Impact (via cost-effectiveness)


What evaluators expect (at a glance)
A clean, readable, internally consistent budget where:
every major cost is traceable to a WP/task

allocations are balanced across partners

nothing looks inflated, hidden, or unexplained

If they can “scan and trust”, you win.

Best-practice structure (recommended)
1. One master table (summary)
Include:
WP

Cost category (management, implementation, meetings, dissemination, etc.)

Partner

Amount

% of total WP / total project

✔ Lets evaluators see balance immediately.

2. WP-level breakdown
For each WP (esp. WP1–WP3):
brief description of what the WP covers

table mapping:

tasks → partners → costs

totals per WP

✔ Shows task–cost coherence.

3. Partner-level view
For each partner:
total allocation

breakdown by WP

brief note on role intensity

✔ Confirms fair workload distribution.

4. Cost logic notes (very short)
Add 1–2 lines per WP explaining:
why the amount is proportionate

key drivers (duration, complexity, partner count)

✔ Prevents “why is this so high?” questions.

What evaluators reward implicitly
Proportionality

WP1 usually ~10–20% (rarely >25%)

No WP dominating without reason

Consistency

Big tasks = visible budget

Active partners = visible allocation

Transparency

No “buffer” lines

No unexplained spikes

Cost-effectiveness

Reuse of competences

Sensible mix of online / physical work

No duplication across WPs


Things to avoid (red flags)
❌ Round numbers everywhere with no logic

❌ WP1 larger than core content WPs

❌ One partner absorbing most of the budget without a strong role

❌ Costs appearing in the budget but not described in the application

❌ Annex introducing new activities not mentioned elsewhere

If the budget tells a different story than the form → problem.

Formatting & presentation tips
Use Excel-style tables (even if uploaded as PDF)

Clear titles: “Detailed Budget – KA220 – Project X”

Consistent naming with application (WP names, partner acronyms)

Avoid dense text blocks

One annex = one message


How to reference it in the application
Use short, functional references such as:
“The allocation reflects the scope of activities described in WP2 (see Detailed Budget, Annex X).”
Never say “see annex for details” to replace an explanation — only to confirm it.

Quick evaluator self-check (before upload)
Ask yourself:
Can I explain every big number in one sentence?

Does effort = money = role?

Does anything look like a comfort margin?

If yes → safe.
 If not → trim or rebalance.
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T116R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Annex: MONITORING PLAN

Purpose for evaluators
To verify that the project has a clear, structured and realistic system to monitor:
progress of activities
quality of outputs
achievement of objectives
and that monitoring results are used for decision-making and improvement.
This annex mainly reinforces:
Quality of project design and implementation
Impact (credibility of results)
Risk prevention and feasibility

What evaluators expect to see
At a glance, evaluators should see that:
monitoring is planned, regular and shared
responsibilities are clearly assigned
indicators and tools are appropriate and proportional
monitoring is continuous, not only final
They are not looking for a heavy QA system, but for control + learning.

Recommended structure (best practice)
1. Monitoring framework overview
Short explanation of:
what is monitored (activities, outputs, objectives)
at which levels (WP, project-wide)
Clarify that monitoring supports:
progress tracking
quality assurance
corrective action

2. Roles and responsibilities
Clearly define:
Project coordinator
Overall monitoring, consolidation of data, reporting to NA.
WP leaders
Monitoring progress and quality within their WP.
Partner organisations
Self-monitoring of tasks, contribution to reviews.
(If relevant) Associated partners
Feedback or validation role only.
Avoid concentration of all monitoring on one person.

3. Indicators and monitoring criteria
Describe:
Quantitative indicators
milestones achieved
deliverables completed
participation levels (where relevant)
Qualitative indicators
relevance and usability of outputs
coherence with objectives
feedback from target groups and partners
Indicators should be:
linked to objectives and WPs
limited in number
realistic to collect

4. Monitoring tools and methods
Specify practical tools, such as:
WP work plans and timelines
progress reports from partners
internal peer review of outputs
feedback forms or validation sessions
coordination meetings with monitoring as a standing agenda item
Avoid overly technical or external QA systems unless justified.

5. Timing and frequency
Clearly indicate:
continuous monitoring during implementation
periodic reviews (e.g. monthly or per WP milestone)
key monitoring moments:
mid-term review
pre-deliverable validation
final project assessment
Regularity is more important than frequency.

6. Use of monitoring results
Explain how monitoring outcomes are used to:
adjust activities or timelines
improve quality of outputs
address delays or risks early
inform reporting and follow-up actions
Monitoring must clearly feed into decisions.

What to avoid (red flags)
Vague statements like “the project will be monitored regularly”
Monitoring only at reporting stage
Indicators not linked to objectives
No explanation of who does what
Overly complex systems disproportionate to the project

What increases evaluator confidence
Clear division of monitoring responsibilities
Simple but structured monitoring logic
Evidence-based indicators
Monitoring embedded in coordination routines
Explicit link between monitoring and quality improvement
```

Full content [T117R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Annex: EVALUATION PLAN

Purpose for evaluators
To demonstrate that the project has a structured, evidence-based evaluation system to assess:
achievement of objectives
quality of results
short- and medium-term impact
and to ensure learning and improvement throughout the project.
This annex mainly reinforces:
Impact
Quality of project design and implementation
Credibility of results and sustainability

What evaluators expect to see
At a glance, evaluators should see that:
evaluation is planned and intentional
it goes beyond monitoring (focus on effects and quality)
responsibilities and timing are clear
results are used for improvement and validation

Recommended structure (best practice)
1. Evaluation approach and scope
Briefly explain that evaluation focuses on:
achievement of project and WP objectives
quality and relevance of results
effects on organisations and target groups
Clarify the difference between:
monitoring (progress control)
evaluation (assessment of results and impact)

2. Evaluation questions
Define a limited set of core questions, such as:
To what extent were the project objectives achieved?
How relevant and usable are the project results for target groups?
What changes did the project generate for participating organisations?
What added value emerged from transnational cooperation?
Evaluation questions should guide data collection.

3. Evaluation criteria and indicators
Describe the main criteria used, including:
Effectiveness (objectives achieved)
Quality (relevance, usability, coherence of results)
Impact (changes for organisations and target groups)
Sustainability potential (continued use of results)
For each criterion, combine:
quantitative indicators (outputs delivered, uptake, participation)
qualitative indicators (feedback, validation outcomes, reflections)

4. Evaluation methods and data sources
Specify realistic and proportionate methods, such as:
analysis of WP results and deliverables
feedback from partners and target groups
peer review of outputs
reflection sessions within the partnership
final evaluation questionnaires or interviews
Avoid overly academic or external-heavy methods unless necessary.

5. Roles and responsibilities
Clearly define who is responsible for:
coordinating the evaluation process (project coordinator)
contributing data and reflections (partners and WP leaders)
validating results (partnership-level review)
Evaluation should be shared, not centralised only.

6. Timing and evaluation phases
Describe evaluation moments, for example:
baseline reflection (early phase)
mid-term evaluation (progress and early effects)
final evaluation (achievement, quality, impact)
follow-up reflection on sustainability
Timing should align with WP milestones.

7. Use of evaluation results
Explain how evaluation findings are used to:
improve project implementation
validate final results
inform dissemination and exploitation
support sustainability and follow-up actions
Evaluation must clearly inform decisions and learning.

What to avoid (red flags)
Confusing evaluation with monitoring
Generic statements without criteria
Evaluation only at the end of the project
No link between evaluation and decision-making
Overly complex or unrealistic evaluation systems

What increases evaluator confidence
Clear evaluation questions
Balanced qualitative and quantitative approach
Proportionate and feasible methods
Evaluation integrated into the project lifecycle
Strong link between evaluation, impact and sustainability
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T118R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Annex: RISK MANAGEMENT PLAN

Purpose for evaluators
To demonstrate that the partnership has anticipated potential risks, assessed their likelihood and impact, and defined realistic mitigation measures to ensure smooth implementation and achievement of results.
This annex mainly reinforces:
Quality of project design and implementation
Quality of partnership and cooperation
Feasibility and reliability of the project

What evaluators expect to see
At a glance, evaluators should see that:
risks are identified proactively, not defensively
mitigation measures are concrete and feasible
responsibilities are clearly assigned
the project is resilient and adaptable
They do not expect zero risk, but risk awareness and control.

Recommended structure (best practice)
1. Risk management approach
Brief explanation of:
how risks were identified (partner reflection, past experience)
how risk management is integrated into project coordination
Clarify that risk management is:
ongoing
reviewed regularly during implementation

2. Risk identification
Identify the main categories of risks, for example:
Implementation risks
(delays in tasks, workload imbalance, dependency between WPs)
Partnership risks
(reduced engagement, staff changes, communication issues)
Quality risks
(outputs not meeting expected standards, limited usability)
External risks
(contextual changes, availability of participants, unforeseen constraints)
Avoid listing too many minor risks.

3. Risk assessment
For each key risk, briefly indicate:
likelihood (low / medium / high)
potential impact on the project
WP or project phase affected
Assessment should be realistic, not alarmist.

4. Mitigation and prevention measures
Describe specific mitigation actions, such as:
clear task allocation and deadlines
regular coordination and monitoring meetings
internal peer review and quality checks
backup solutions (alternative formats, adjusted timelines)
redistribution of tasks if needed
Measures must be actionable, not generic.

5. Roles and responsibilities
Clearly define:
who monitors risks (coordinator, WP leaders)
how partners report emerging issues
how mitigation decisions are taken
Risk management should be shared, not centralised only.

6. Review and adaptation
Explain how risks are:
reviewed periodically
updated if new risks emerge
addressed through corrective actions
Link this to monitoring and coordination routines.

What to avoid (red flags)
Claiming that no risks are expected
Generic risks with generic mitigation
No ownership of risks
Overly pessimistic or overly optimistic tone
Risk plan disconnected from monitoring and coordination

What increases evaluator confidence
Focus on realistic, relevant risks
Clear link between risks and mitigation measures
Shared responsibility across the partnership
Risk management embedded in project governance
Evidence of learning from past projects
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

```text
Annex: DISSEMINATION TABLE

Purpose for evaluators
To verify that dissemination is planned, structured and proportionate, and that results will reach relevant audiences through appropriate channels, at the right time.
This annex mainly reinforces:
Impact
Quality of project design and implementation
Sustainability and exploitation of results

What evaluators expect to see
At a glance, evaluators should see that:
dissemination is strategic, not generic
actions are linked to specific results and target groups
responsibilities and timing are clear
dissemination goes beyond visibility and supports uptake

Recommended table structure (best practice)
Each row should represent one dissemination action.
Recommended columns:
Project result/output
Target group(s)
Key message / purpose
Dissemination channel(s)
Format (event, post, publication, workshop, etc.)
Timing / project phase
Responsible partner(s)
Expected reach or indicator
✔ This structure allows evaluators to assess logic quickly.

How to fill the table effectively
1. Start from results, not channels
Each action should be linked to:
a specific project output or result
Avoid actions that exist “just to communicate”
✔ Result-driven dissemination scores higher.

2. Match target groups and channels
Clearly differentiate:
end users
multipliers
decision-makers or stakeholders
Choose channels they actually use
✔ One-size-fits-all dissemination is a red flag.

3. Clarify the purpose of each action
Specify whether the action aims to:
inform
engage
validate
support uptake or reuse
✔ Dissemination ≠ promotion only.

4. Show timing and progression
Cover different phases:
early (awareness, engagement)
mid-term (validation, testing)
final (uptake, transfer)
Avoid clustering everything at the end
✔ Phased dissemination shows maturity.

5. Assign clear responsibility
Always indicate:
which partner leads
which partners support (if relevant)
Ensure roles reflect expertise and networks
✔ Responsibility = accountability.

Indicators (keep them realistic)
Use simple, meaningful indicators, such as:
number of events or workshops
number of organisations reached
number of downloads or accesses
qualitative feedback or uptake signals
Avoid inflated reach figures without justification.

What to avoid (red flags)
Generic actions repeated across rows
Same target group and channel for all outputs
Dissemination actions not mentioned in the application
Over-reliance on social media alone
Confusing dissemination with sustainability

What increases evaluator confidence
Clear link: result → target group → action
Variety of channels, used strategically
Phased and purposeful dissemination
Realistic indicators
Strong coherence with the impact and sustainability sections
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

```text
Annex: GANTT CHART

Purpose for evaluators
To verify that the project has a clear, realistic and well-sequenced timeline, showing:
logical progression of WPs, tasks and activities
feasibility within the project duration
alignment between timing, responsibilities and results
This annex mainly reinforces:
Quality of project design and implementation
Feasibility and risk management
Budget and time coherence

What evaluators expect to see
At a glance, evaluators should see that:
the project timeline is logical and balanced
dependencies between WPs are clear
no WP or task is overloaded or unrealistic
milestones and deliverables are identifiable
They use the Gantt to answer: “Can this actually be done in time?”

Recommended structure (best practice)
1. Timeline overview
Horizontal axis: months of the project
Vertical axis:
Work Packages
Main tasks / activities per WP
Clearly mark:
start and end of each WP
overlaps where cooperation is needed

2. Work Package sequencing
WP1 (Management): runs throughout the project
Content / development WPs:
start after needs analysis or preparation
feed logically into testing, dissemination or exploitation WPs
Avoid all WPs starting at month 1 unless justified

3. Tasks and milestones
For each WP, include:
main tasks (not micro-activities)
key milestones or review points
Deliverables should:
appear at realistic moments
not be clustered only at the very end

4. Dependencies and logic
Make visible:
which tasks depend on previous outputs
which WPs enable others
Use overlaps to show:
validation
feedback loops
iterative improvement

5. Partner involvement (if included)
Optionally indicate:
WP leader
main contributing partners
Keep this readable and not overcrowded

Alignment checks evaluators perform
Timeline ↔ WP descriptions
Timeline ↔ budget distribution
Timeline ↔ monitoring and evaluation moments
Timeline ↔ dissemination phases
Any contradiction raises doubts.

What to avoid (red flags)
Overcrowded Gantt with excessive detail
All deliverables in the last month
Long WPs with no intermediate milestones
No overlap between development and validation
Timeline that contradicts the application narrative

What increases evaluator confidence
Clear WP flow and dependencies
Realistic pacing of tasks
Visible review and validation moments
Balanced distribution of workload over time
Strong coherence with budget, monitoring and risk plans
```

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T121R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Annex: IMPACT ASSESSMENT FRAMEWORK

Purpose for evaluators
To demonstrate that the project has a clear, structured and credible framework to assess:
achievement of objectives
effects on target groups and organisations
wider and longer-term impact
and that impact is measured, evidenced and used, not only claimed.
This annex mainly reinforces:
Impact
Quality of project design and implementation
Sustainability and European added value

What evaluators expect to see
At a glance, evaluators should see that:
impact is defined, not assumed
assessment goes beyond outputs and activities
indicators are relevant, realistic and measurable
impact assessment is linked to decision-making and learning
They are looking for causal logic, not inflated ambition.

Recommended structure (best practice)
1. Impact logic and levels
Briefly explain the project’s impact logic, distinguishing between:
Outputs (what is produced)
Outcomes (changes in practice, capacity or behaviour)
Impact (longer-term effects at organisational or systemic level)
Clarify impact levels:
target groups
participating organisations
wider ecosystem (local / national / European)

2. Impact objectives
List the main impact objectives, aligned with project objectives, such as:
improved organisational capacity or practices
enhanced competences or opportunities for target groups
stronger cooperation or transfer of practices across countries
Objectives should describe change, not activities.

3. Impact indicators
Define a limited and balanced set of indicators, combining:
Quantitative indicators
uptake or use of results
number of organisations applying outputs
replication or continuation actions
participation or reach beyond the partnership
Qualitative indicators
perceived relevance and usefulness of results
changes in organisational practices
feedback on quality, applicability and value
evidence of learning or transformation
Indicators must be:
clearly linked to impact objectives
feasible to collect within the project lifecycle

4. Data sources and methods
Describe how impact data is collected, for example through:
partner and target group feedback
validation and reflection sessions
analysis of use and integration of results
follow-up surveys or qualitative reporting
Avoid overly academic or heavy methodologies.

5. Timing of impact assessment
Explain when impact is assessed:
during implementation (early and intermediate effects)
at project end (achievement of outcomes)
where relevant, shortly after completion (initial sustainability signals)
Timing should align with WP milestones and evaluation moments.

6. Roles and responsibilities
Clearly define:
who coordinates the impact assessment
how partners contribute data and analysis
how findings are discussed and validated at partnership level
Impact assessment should be shared, not centralised only.

7. Use of impact findings
Explain how impact results are used to:
validate project success
improve final outputs
inform dissemination and exploitation
support sustainability and future initiatives
Impact assessment must clearly feed into action and learning.

What to avoid (red flags)
Confusing impact with dissemination reach
Only output-based indicators
Impact claims without measurement
Overly ambitious or policy-level claims without mechanisms
Impact assessment detached from evaluation and monitoring

What increases evaluator confidence
Clear impact logic and definitions
Strong link between objectives, outcomes and indicators
Balanced qualitative and quantitative evidence
Realistic scope of impact
Impact assessment integrated into the project lifecycle
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

INDICATORS AND RESULTS TABLE

### Summary structure of the answer / elements to prepare

1. Project/WP objective of each line
2. Risultato e tipo output/outcome
3. Indicatori quantitativi
4. Qualitative indicators and quality criterion
5. Source/method of verification
6. Tempi e responsabile
7. Baseline e target motivati da aggiungere in curatela

The structure is an editorial checklist: it does not add eligibility requirements. Adapt to the relevant partner/WP/business instance.

### Pianificazione privata

- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Project/WP objective of each line
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Result and output/outcome type
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Quantitative indicators
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Qualitative indicators and quality criterion
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Source/method of verification
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Times and responsible
- [ ] Collect data and evidence for: Baselines and motivated targets to add in curation
- [ ] Report missing data and verify crucial sources before making commitments.
- [ ] Check consistency with application, other sections, calendar, roles and budget.

This is an operational checklist of evidence and completeness; it does not require expounding or memorizing internal reasoning.

### Critica e verifica richiesta

Each row is a result, not an activity. The baseline and targets are curation integrations, not columns in the box.

Note di curatela:

- Attachment suggested by the model; mandatory, uploadability and file size not confirmed.

### Good practices, examples and specific content of the tables

Full content [T122R1C1], **non-regulatory**. The model sentences are examples to be replaced with verified data; Score recommendations and claims do not prove an effect on the rating.

```text
Annex: INDICATORS AND RESULTS TABLE

Purpose for evaluators
To verify that the project has a clear, coherent and measurable results framework, linking:
objectives
results
indicators
evidence sources
This annex mainly reinforces:
Quality of project design and implementation
Impact
Credibility of monitoring and evaluation

What evaluators expect to see
At a glance, evaluators should see that:
every key objective has corresponding results
each result is linked to clear indicators
indicators are measurable and realistic
data sources and responsibilities are identifiable
They use this table to check logic and accountability.

Recommended table structure (best practice)
Each row should represent one result (not one activity).
Recommended columns:
Project / WP objective
Expected result
Type of result (output / outcome)
Quantitative indicators
Qualitative indicators
Data source / verification method
Timing (when measured)
Responsible partner(s)
✔ This structure allows fast validation of coherence.

How to populate the table effectively
1. Start from objectives
Each result must clearly derive from:
a project objective or
a WP objective
Avoid results that are not anchored to objectives
✔ Objective → result → indicator must be traceable.

2. Define results clearly
Results should be:
concrete and identifiable
phrased as “what exists or changes”
not activities or intentions
✔ Example: “Validated framework” ≠ “Framework development”.

3. Use balanced indicators
For each result, include:
Quantitative indicators
completion or delivery status
uptake, use or replication
participation or implementation figures
Qualitative indicators
relevance and usability
quality and coherence
perceived value by users or partners
Avoid vanity metrics and inflated numbers.

4. Specify verification sources
Clearly indicate how indicators are measured, e.g.:
deliverables
internal reports
feedback forms
validation sessions
partner confirmations
✔ Evidence matters more than numbers.

5. Timing and responsibility
Indicate when indicators are measured:
mid-term
per milestone
final
Assign responsibility clearly
✔ Prevents “who checks this?” doubts.

What to avoid (red flags)
Indicators not linked to results
Only quantitative indicators
Measuring activities instead of results
Indicators impossible to verify
Same indicators copied across results

What increases evaluator confidence
Clear objective–result–indicator logic
Limited, meaningful indicators
Balanced qualitative and quantitative evidence
Realistic measurement approach
Strong coherence with monitoring and evaluation plans
```

Examples identified in the box: stored in the JSON as `illustrative_starter_not_project_fact` or `illustration_not_verified_fact_or_requirement`. They do not constitute data, experiences or project choices.

## Completezza e riuso

PartnerP1–P7, WP2–WP6 occurrences, and timesheets remain separately addressable. Repeating a pattern does not allow you to replicate data. The full extraction also preserves legends, working links, blank lines, dropzone instructions, historical references, and any text not classified as a question.

Before the real application: identify year, round, sector, agency and current version; Compare the field topology and limits with the actual form. No automatic transfer of priorities, annexes, limits or criteria to centralized ESC or Sport.
